The catalytic loop residues (aspartates) coordinate these metals.<br><br>Evaluating the choices:<br>- A: Ca2+ is not typically used by kinases as a catalytic cofactor (it&#x27;s too large and doesn&#x27;t coordinate well in the active site geometry)<br>- B: Mn2+ and Ni2+ are not used &quot;equally&quot; — Ni2+ is typically less efficient<br>- C: Zn2+ is not the sole metal used by kinases of this type; Mg2+ and Mn2+ are the primary physiological cofactors<br>- D: This matches the known biochemistry of DCLK kinases — Mg2+ and Mn2+ support catalytic activity with similar efficiency, while Ni2+ can partially substitute but with reduced activity<br><br>The answer is D.<br><br>2. answer: D</td>

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which catalytic activity best describes this protein?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Cleaves type-I transmembrane domains by intramembrane proteolysis, using a serine–histidine catalytic dyad contributed by different transmembrane segments.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Cleaves soluble proteins using an aspartyl-protease catalytic dyad, with hydrolysis occurring outside the membrane.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Cuts extracellular peptide bonds using a zinc-dependent metalloprotease active site.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Degrades cytosolic proteins using a cysteine–histidine catalytic dyad, without cleaving membrane-embedded substrates.<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: Analyzing the protein sequence, I can identify several key features:<br><br>**Membrane topology**: The sequence contains multiple hydrophobic stretches (approximately 6-7 transmembrane segments), which is characteristic of membrane-embedded enzymes rather than soluble proteins.
